It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.

But when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.

It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.

People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.

The strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.

But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.

I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.

Like, I’ll look up routes ahead of time and take the long way just so I don’t have to drive over them. And if there’s no way around it, I can do it, but it’s awful. I’m gripping the wheel the whole time, tense the entire way across, just trying to get through it. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.

I mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.

So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.

My chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.

I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.

It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.\n\nI know the bridge is built fine. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really like that. If I’m at home or just somewhere else, I don’t walk around feeling scared of high bridges out of nowhere. I can think about them and know, you know, those bridges over the bay are built fine — people drive them every day, back and forth, all day long. I understand that.

It’s when I’m actually getting near one, in the car, that my mind starts doing that thing where it acts like the car could go right over the side. That’s the part that feels real to me in the moment. Away from the car and away from the bridge, it’s more just a thought, not the same fear.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I came in is that I’ve started planning my whole day around avoiding certain roads—specifically those high bridges. Like, I’ll look up routes ahead of time and take the long way just so I don’t have to drive over them. And if there’s no way around it, I can do it, but it’s awful. I’m gripping the wheel the whole time, tense the entire way across, just trying to get through it. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.\n\nI know the bridge is built fine. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're not in a vehicle at all, such as when you're at home or in a completely different setting?\n\nPatient: No, not really like that. If I’m at home or just somewhere else, I don’t walk around feeling scared of high bridges out of nowhere. I can think about them and know, you know, those bridges over the bay are built fine — people drive them every day, back and forth, all day long. I understand that.\n\nIt’s when I’m actually getting near one, in the car, that my mind starts doing that thing where it acts like the car could go right over the side. That’s the part that feels real to me in the moment. Away from the car and away from the bridge, it’s more just a thought, not the same fear.\n\n[Last Question]\nDoes your fear of going over the side occur when you are on a bicycle or motorcycle crossing over a high bridge?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / human_resources_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and folks drive them every day, but when I get near one my mind acts like the car could go right over the side.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really ride a motorcycle, and I’m not usually on a bicycle over those kinds of bridges, so I can’t say from experience the same way. For me it’s mostly tied to being in the car and coming up on one of those high bridges over the bay.

Like I’ve said, I know in my head they’re built fine, and I know people drive over them all day, every day. But when I get near one, my mind just starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the image or fear that pops in. It’s really the car-on-the-bridge situation that does it for me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I came in is that I’ve started planning my whole day around avoiding certain roads—specifically those high bridges. Like, I’ll look up routes ahead of time and take the long way just so I don’t have to drive over them. And if there’s no way around it, I can do it, but it’s awful. I’m gripping the wheel the whole time, tense the entire way across, just trying to get through it. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.\n\nI know the bridge is built fine. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're not in a vehicle at all, such as when you're at home or in a completely different setting?\n\nPatient: No, not really like that. If I’m at home or just somewhere else, I don’t walk around feeling scared of high bridges out of nowhere. I can think about them and know, you know, those bridges over the bay are built fine — people drive them every day, back and forth, all day long. I understand that.\n\nIt’s when I’m actually getting near one, in the car, that my mind starts doing that thing where it acts like the car could go right over the side. That’s the part that feels real to me in the moment. Away from the car and away from the bridge, it’s more just a thought, not the same fear.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side occur when you are on a bicycle or motorcycle crossing over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really ride a motorcycle, and I’m not usually on a bicycle over those kinds of bridges, so I can’t say from experience the same way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can’t really say it comes up the same way just standing or walking on a bridge. For me, the thing that really sets it off is being in the car and getting near one of those high bridges over the bay.

I know, sitting here talking about it, that those bridges are built fine. I know folks drive over them every single day and nothing happens. But when I’m actually approaching one in a car, my mind starts going to that place where it feels like the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the part that grabs me. Walking or standing there isn’t really the main thing I’ve noticed—it’s the car being up there on the bridge.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I came in is that I’ve started planning my whole day around avoiding certain roads—specifically those high bridges. Like, I’ll look up routes ahead of time and take the long way just so I don’t have to drive over them. And if there’s no way around it, I can do it, but it’s awful. I’m gripping the wheel the whole time, tense the entire way across, just trying to get through it. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're not in a vehicle at all, such as when you're at home or in a completely different setting?\n\nPatient: No, not really like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I can think about high bridges when I’m not on one, but it doesn’t hit me the same way. The thing that seems to set it off is driving toward one of those big bridges over the bay.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… the main reason I came in is that I’ve started planning my whole day around avoiding certain roads—specifically those high bridges. Like, I’ll look up routes ahead of time and take the long way just so I don’t have to drive over them. And if there’s no way around it, I can do it, but it’s awful. I’m gripping the wheel the whole time, tense the entire way across, just trying to get through it. It’s gotten to the point where it feels like it’s running more of my day than it should.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges themselves that bothers you, or is it more about driving on them?\n\nPatient: It’s… I guess it’s more the height and being up over the water, but it hits me through the driving, if that makes sense. Like, I know those bridges over the bay are built solid. I know thousands of people drive over them every day and nothing happens. I can tell myself that.\n\nBut when I’m coming up on one, my mind just goes straight to this picture of the car somehow going over the side. Not that I really believe the bridge is unsafe, exactly, it’s just this awful feeling like there’s nothing between me and that drop, and I’m the one having to keep the car steady. So it’s not regular driving that bothers me—it’s driving on those high bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar feeling of fear or anxiety when you're in other high places, like tall buildings or mountain edges, or is it primarily just the driving on high bridges that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the bridges, especially the high ones over the bay. I mean, I can be aware of height in other places, sure, but what really sets me off is when I’m in the car and I’m the one driving across. I know, logically, those bridges are built fine. People drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens.\n\nBut when I get close to one, it’s like my head stops caring about all that. It starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, like one wrong move and that’s it. I can tell myself, “This is safe, the bridge is solid,” but my body doesn’t seem to believe it once I’m up there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're a passenger in a car driving over a high bridge, or is the fear mostly when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when I’m driving, I’d say. Being a passenger over one of those high bridges isn’t exactly pleasant either, but the worst part is when I’m the one behind the wheel, because then it feels like it’s on me to keep the car steady.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds a little irrational, because I do know those bridges over the bay are built fine. I mean, people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. I know that in my head. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind just doesn’t stay with that. It goes straight to this thought, or almost like a picture, that the car could somehow go right over the side. So even if I’m telling myself, “You’re safe, just keep driving,” my body reacts like there’s real danger right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar anxiety when operating other vehicles or machinery that require precision or control, such as a boat or an airplane?\n\nPatient: Not really with other things, no. I mean, I don’t fly planes or anything like that, and I’m not out operating heavy machinery. It’s really tied to those bridges over the bay and being in the car up there.\n\nThe strange part is, I can sit here and tell you I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.\n\nI know the bridge is built fine. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're not in a vehicle at all, such as when you're at home or in a completely different setting?\n\nPatient: No, not really like that. I know people drive them every day, all day long, and it’s not like cars are just going over the edge. But when I get near one, it’s like my mind doesn’t go with what I know. It just jumps to this picture that the car could somehow go right over the side. And because I’m the one driving, it feels like every little movement matters too much, even though I know that’s not really how it works.\n\nDoctor: Does the anxiety you experience when driving over high bridges ever occur in situations where you're not driving, but still have some form of control over your movement or environment?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. It seems pretty tied to the car and the bridge for me. Like, if I’m walking somewhere or just moving around on my own, I don’t get that same thing in the same way.\n\nIt’s really when I’m getting near one of those high bridges over the bay, especially in the car, that my mind starts doing that. I know the bridges are built fine, and I know people drive over them every single day without anything happening. I can say that out loud and believe it sitting here. But once I’m near it, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side, even though I know that doesn’t make sense. That’s the situation where it really grabs me.\n\nDoctor: Does the fear of going over the side occur when you're a pedestrian on a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have it that way as a pedestrian, at least not that I can clearly point to. The fear I keep talking about is much more tied to being in the car near those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them all day, every day, and nothing happens. But when I’m coming up on one in the car, my mind just sort of flips into this idea that the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the picture that gets stuck in my head. Walking on a bridge isn’t really the situation that brings it on like that for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when you are a passenger on a bus or train that crosses over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say it’s closest when I’m in a car. A bus or a train isn’t really the main thing I think of with it. What happens for me is more when I’m coming up on one of those bridges over the bay and I’m in the car, especially if I’m the one driving.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, those bridges are built fine. I know people go over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. But when I get near one, my mind kind of takes off on its own and starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not really a sensible thought, but it feels real in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side ever occur when you're in control of a vehicle or situation other than driving a car over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Not really in any other situation that I can think of. It’s pretty specific for me — it’s those high bridges over the bay, and being in the car.\n\nI mean, I can sit here and tell you I know the bridges are built fine. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and they’re okay. But when I get close to one, my mind just starts acting like the car could go right over the side. It’s not like I’m having that same thought when I’m controlling some other machine or in some other place. It’s really that car-on-the-bridge situation that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges ever triggered by just thinking about driving over one, even when you're not actually in a car?\n\nPatient: Thinking about it can make me uneasy, but the real thing — the part where it really hits me — is when I actually see the bridge coming up. Like, if I’m in the car and I can see it ahead, that’s when my body reacts right away.\n\nMy chest gets tight pretty much as soon as I notice it, and my hands start sweating on the steering wheel. Then I get this kind of tunnel focus where I’m just staring straight ahead, like, “Just keep going, just get to the other side.” I’m not really thinking normally at that point. It’s like I’m locked onto the road and trying to get across without looking around too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of driving over high bridges lessened at all if someone else is driving you over the bridge, but you're still in the car?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it doesn’t really go away. If someone else is driving, I’m not having to hold the wheel, so in that sense I can kind of tell myself, “Just sit here, let them drive.” But once we get near one of those bridges over the bay, my mind still starts doing that same thing.\n\nI know the bridge is built fine. I know people drive over it all day long and nothing happens. I can say that logically. But when we’re actually close to it, even as a passenger, I still get that thought like the car could somehow go right over the side. It’s not as much about who’s driving as it is being in the car on that high bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're not in a vehicle at all, such as when you're at home or in a completely different setting?\n\nPatient: No, not really like that. If I’m at home or just somewhere else, I don’t walk around feeling scared of high bridges out of nowhere. I can think about them and know, you know, those bridges over the bay are built fine — people drive them every day, back and forth, all day long. I understand that.\n\nIt’s when I’m actually getting near one, in the car, that my mind starts doing that thing where it acts like the car could go right over the side. That’s the part that feels real to me in the moment. Away from the car and away from the bridge, it’s more just a thought, not the same fear.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of going over the side occur when you are on a bicycle or motorcycle crossing over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: I don’t really ride a motorcycle, and I’m not usually on a bicycle over those kinds of bridges, so I can’t say from experience the same way. For me it’s mostly tied to being in the car and coming up on one of those high bridges over the bay.\n\nLike I’ve said, I know in my head they’re built fine, and I know people drive over them all day, every day. But when I get near one, my mind just starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side. That’s the image or fear that pops in. It’s really the car-on-the-bridge situation that does it for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever present when you're walking or standing on a bridge, not just driving over it?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say it comes up the same way just standing or walking on a bridge. For me, the thing that really sets it off is being in the car and getting near one of those high bridges over the bay.\n\nI know, sitting here talking about it, that those bridges are built fine. I know folks drive over them every single day and nothing happens. Walking or standing there isn’t really the main thing I’ve noticed—it’s the car being up there on the bridge.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of high bridges ever triggered by anything else besides driving over them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can think about high bridges when I’m not on one, but it doesn’t hit me the same way. The thing that seems to set it off is driving toward one of those big bridges over the bay.\n\nIt’s strange because I know they’re built right. I know people drive over them every day, all day long, and nothing happens. I can tell myself that sitting here. Yeah… it’s not just the driving part, like the steering or traffic or anything like that. It’s really the moment I see the bridge coming up. As soon as I spot it ahead of me, even before I’m actually on it, I can feel my chest get tight, and my hands start sweating on the wheel.
